A constant \(C_{\epsilon}^{\mathrm{fb}}>0\) therefore satisfies \[M^2 \le C_{\epsilon}^{\mathrm{fb}}M^2 \left\{\frac1n+\min\{1,u_{n,d}\}\right\}\] throughout the inactive branch. Taking the final risk constant to dominate both \(C_{\epsilon}^{\mathrm{fb}}\) and \(D_\epsilon+1\) proves the all-alphabet risk bound.

∎

Lemma 13 turns the light-cell analysis into a usable estimator bound. The zero fallback in the inactive range keeps the estimator total for every \(n,d\), and the displayed risk bound gives the polynomial scale used by the selector in Algorithm 3. The operation count records that, after aggregation by cell and order, the implementation grows polynomially in the alphabet size and degree.

The finite-range construction theorem combines the polynomial and collision branches before the all-alphabet packaging in Theorem 1. It keeps the calibrated active range explicit, which is useful for downstream statements that invoke the original range condition.

Theorem 6 [thm:robust-upper-construction-resolution] (Robust upper construction).

For every overlap parameter \(0<\epsilon<1/2\), there exist constants \(C_{\epsilon},c_{\epsilon}>0\) and a calibrated polynomial handle consisting of a cutoff \(N\) and an active-range multiplier \(\rho>0\) such that, for every \(n,d\ge 1\), \[N\le n,\qquad d\le \rho n\log(en)\] implies that the associated polynomial degree is at least \(2\). For every \(n,d\ge 1\), \(M\ge 1\), and \(0\le \sigma\le 2\), the handle-indexed polynomial estimator \(T_n^{\mathrm{poly}}\) and the occupancy-weighted estimator \(T_n^{\mathrm{col}}\) of Algorithm 2 are measurable functions of the sample and take values in \([-M,M]\).

If, in addition, \[d\le c_{\epsilon}\frac{n^2}{\log(en)},\] then for every \(P\in\mathcal P_{d,\epsilon,M,\sigma}\) as defined in Definition 1, \[E_{Q_P^{(n)}}\!\left[\left(T_n^{\mathrm{poly}}-\tau(P)\right)^2\right] \le C_{\epsilon}M^2 \left\{ \frac1n+\min\left(1,\frac{d^2}{n^2\log^2(en)}\right) \right\},\] and \[E_{Q_P^{(n)}}\!\left[\left(T_n^{\mathrm{col}}-\tau(P)\right)^2\right] \le C_{\epsilon}M^2 \left( \frac1n+\sigma^2+\frac{d}{n^2} \right).\]

⊢ Lean

Theorem 6 states the two estimators on the same calibrated range. The polynomial branch contributes the large-alphabet approximation rate, and the occupancy branch contributes the radius-sensitive collision rate. Since both estimators are clipped into \([-M,M]\), the selector can compare their deterministic benchmark terms within the admissible class of Definition 5.

The final result in this appendix is the restricted selector bound. It is the finite-range version of the selector statement used in the main text and follows by applying the branch with the smaller displayed nonparametric benchmark.

Theorem 7 [thm:frontier-upper] (Restricted frontier upper bound).

For every overlap parameter \(\epsilon\) with \(0<\epsilon<1/2\), there exist constants \(C_{\epsilon},c_{\epsilon}>0\) and a polynomial calibration handle \((N,\rho)\) with \(\rho>0\) such that, for all positive integers \(n,d\), \[N\le n \quad\text{and}\quad d\le \rho n\log(en) \quad\Longrightarrow\quad 2\le K_n ,\] where \(K_n\) is the active polynomial degree in the polynomial estimator. For every \(n,d,M,\sigma\), assume:

  • (Sample and alphabet.) \(n\ge1\) and \(d\ge1\).

  • (Envelope and radius.) \(M\ge1\) and \(0\le\sigma\le2\).

  • (Restricted dimension.) \[d\le c_{\epsilon}\frac{n^2}{\log(en)} .\]

Let \(T_n^{\mathrm{poly}}\in\mathcal T_{n,M}\) be the polynomial estimator determined by this handle, let \(T_n^{\mathrm{col}}\in\mathcal T_{n,M}\) be the collision estimator, and let \(\widehat\tau_n^{\star}\) be the known-radius selector of Algorithm 3 applied to \(T_n^{\mathrm{poly}}\), \(T_n^{\mathrm{col}}\), and \(\sigma\). Put \[r_{n,d,\sigma} = \frac1n+ \min\left\{ 1,\, \min\left\{ \frac{d^2}{n^2\log^2(en)},\, \sigma^2+\frac{d}{n^2} \right\} \right\}.\] Then \(\widehat\tau_n^{\star}\in\mathcal T_{n,M}\), and for every \(P\in\mathcal P_{d,\epsilon,M,\sigma}\) in Definition 1, \[E_{Q_P^{(n)}}\!\left[ \left(\widehat\tau_n^{\star}-\tau(P)\right)^2 \right] \le C_{\epsilon}M^2 r_{n,d,\sigma}.\]

⊢ Lean

Theorem 7 records the finite-range selector guarantee behind the all-alphabet statement in Theorem 2. The benchmark \(r_{n,d,\sigma}\) combines the parametric term with the smallest of the clipped-zero, polynomial, and collision remainders. This is the constructive upper-bound input used when the main bracket is assembled in Theorem 4.

Proofs for lower bounds and regime algebra

This appendix records the auxiliary lower-bound and algebraic statements that support the main bracket. The proof has four mechanisms. The parametric lower bound supplies the \(M^2/n\) sampling term. The exact-homogeneity transport supplies the capped collision baseline \(M^2\{n^{-1}+\min(1,d/n^2)\}\). The radius-channel transport supplies the rare-cell term \(M^2\sigma^2\min\{1,u_{n,d}\}\). The final rate algebra compares these lower benchmarks with the selector benchmark and identifies the matched regimes and residual wedge.

Lemma 14 [lem:parametric-lower] (Parametric minimax lower bound).

For every overlap parameter \(\epsilon\) satisfying \(0<\epsilon<1/2\), there exists a constant \(c_{\epsilon}>0\) such that the following holds. For every \(n,d\in\mathbb N\) and every \(M,\sigma\in\mathbb R\), suppose that

  • (Sample and alphabet sizes.) \(n\ge 1\) and \(d\ge 1\);

  • (Outcome radius.) \(M\ge 1\);

  • (Noise radius.) \(0\le \sigma\le 2\).

Then the minimax mean-squared risk \(\mathsf R_{n,d,\epsilon,M,\sigma}\) of Definition 5 satisfies \[\mathsf R_{n,d,\epsilon,M,\sigma}\ge \frac{c_{\epsilon}M^2}{n}.\]

⊢ Lean
Proof of Lemma 14.

Take \[c_\epsilon:=\frac1{100}.\]

Fix \(n,d,M,\sigma\) satisfying the displayed hypotheses, and choose a cell \(k\in[d]\). Put \[\delta:=\frac{2/5}{\sqrt n}, \qquad u_v:=M(1/2+v\delta-1/2)\quad (v\in\{0,1\}).\] Thus \(u_0=0\) and \(u_1=M\delta\). Moreover, \[\delta^2=\frac{4}{25n}, \qquad 0\le\delta\le\frac25, \qquad |u_0|\le M/2, \qquad |u_1|\le M/2 .\]

For \(v\in\{0,1\}\), let \(P_v\) be the one-cell test law with \(X=k\), \(P(A=1\mid X=k)=1/2\), control potential outcome \(Y(0)=0\), and treated potential outcome \(Y(1)\) distributed on \(\{-M/2,M/2\}\) with mean \(u_v\). Equivalently, draw \(A\) with probability \(1/2\) independently of this treated endpoint and set \(Y=Y(A)\). Thus, for every cell \(\ell\), arm \(a\), and measurable \(S_0,S_1\subseteq\mathbb R\), \[P_v\{X=\ell,A=a,Y(0)\in S_0,Y(1)\in S_1\}\,P_v\{X=\ell\} = P_v\{X=\ell,A=a\}\,P_v\{X=\ell,Y(0)\in S_0,Y(1)\in S_1\},\] so the conditional exchangeability requirement in Definition 1 holds, and consistency holds from \(Y=Y(A)\). The same construction has cell mass one at \(k\), overlap \(1/2\), conditional means \(0\) and \(u_v\), second central moments bounded by \((M/2)^2\le M^2\), and cell deviation \(0\). Together with \(0<\epsilon<1/2\), \(M\ge1\), and \(0\le\sigma\le2\), this gives \[P_v\in\mathcal P_{d,\epsilon,M,\sigma} \qquad (v=0,1).\]

The targets are \[\tau(P_0)=0, \qquad \tau(P_1)=M\delta .\]

Introduce the binary one-cell endpoint pair used for the comparison, and write \(\widetilde Q_v^{(m)}\) for its \(m\)-fold binary observed law under \(v\in\{0,1\}\). Both source laws have the unique cell, propensity \(m_0=1/2\), and control endpoint probability \(g_0=1/2\). The null source law has treated endpoint probability \(g_1=1/2\), while the perturbed source law has treated endpoint probability \(g_1+\delta=1/2+\delta\); this is admissible because \(0\le\delta\) and \(1/2+\delta\le1\). The one-observation chi-square calculation gives \[\chi^2\!\left(\widetilde Q_1^{(1)},\widetilde Q_0^{(1)}\right) = \frac{(1/2)\delta^2}{(1/2)(1-1/2)}.\] The null one-observation atoms are positive, and tensorization gives \[1+ \chi^2\!\left(\widetilde Q_1^{(n)},\widetilde Q_0^{(n)}\right) = \left(1+\frac{(1/2)\delta^2}{(1/2)(1-1/2)}\right)^n \le \exp\!\left( n\frac{(1/2)\delta^2}{(1/2)(1-1/2)} \right).\] Since \[n\, \frac{(1/2)\delta^2}{(1/2)(1-1/2)} = \frac{8}{25} \le \log 2,\] we have \(\chi^2(\widetilde Q_1^{(n)},\widetilde Q_0^{(n)})\le1\). The chi-square-to-total-variation inequality and symmetry of total variation therefore yield \[\operatorname{TV}\!\left(\widetilde Q_0^{(n)},\widetilde Q_1^{(n)}\right) \le \frac12\sqrt{\chi^2\!\left(\widetilde Q_1^{(n)},\widetilde Q_0^{(n)}\right)} \le\frac12 .\] The two real observed \(n\)-sample laws are the corresponding images under the coordinatewise affine map \[(x,a,b)\longmapsto (k,a,\;{\bf 1}\{a=1\}M(b-1/2)).\]

Therefore, by data processing for total variation, \[\operatorname{TV}\!\left(Q_{P_0}^{(n)},Q_{P_1}^{(n)}\right)\le\frac12 .\]

For any measurable estimator \(T\in\mathcal T_{n,M}\), the two-point testing inequality gives \[\frac{(M\delta)^2}{16} \le \max_{v\in\{0,1\}} E_{Q_{P_v}^{(n)}}\!\left[(T-\tau(P_v))^2\right].\]

The supremum over \(\mathcal P_{d,\epsilon,M,\sigma}\) is finite because \(T\in[-M,M]\) and \(|\tau(P)|\le M\) by Lemma 1, so the preceding bound passes through the estimator infimum in Definition 5. Using \(\delta^2=4/(25n)\), \[\mathsf R_{n,d,\epsilon,M,\sigma} \ge \frac{M^2\delta^2}{16} = \frac{M^2}{100n} = \frac{c_\epsilon M^2}{n}.\]

∎

Lemma 14 anchors the lower benchmark at the scale of estimating a bounded real mean from \(n\) observations. This component is combined below with the exact-homogeneity collision scale and the radius-channel rare-cell scale.

The radius-sensitive converse is organized through a channel from binary source experiments into the real-outcome class. The channel preserves the cell and treatment-assignment structure, contracts binary response contrasts by \(\sigma/2\), and then rescales by \(M\).

Definition 12 [synth_5] (Radius-channel hard-family embedding).

For \(0\le\sigma\le2\), set \(\lambda:=\sigma/2\). The embedding \(\Psi\) sends a binary source law from the radius-channel hard family, after padding unused cells with zero mass in the \(d\)-alphabet, to the real-outcome law obtained by the hypothesis-independent channel \[B(a)\longmapsto B'(a)\sim\operatorname{Bernoulli}\!\left\{\frac12+\lambda\left(B(a)-\frac12\right)\right\}, \qquad Y(a):=M\{B'(a)-1/2\}, \qquad Y=Y(A).\] For source laws \(P_0\) and \(P_1\) in the realized family, the embedded targets satisfy \[\tau(\Psi(P_1))-\tau(\Psi(P_0)) = M\frac{\sigma}{2}\{\psi(P_1)-\psi(P_0)\}.\]

Definition 12 is the finite-sample transport map used in the radius-channel lower bound. The target identity shows that a binary separation for \(\psi\) becomes an average-treatment-effect separation with scale \(M\sigma\) in the embedded real-outcome experiment.

The next two transport lemmas calibrate the capped alphabet sizes for the exact-homogeneity and radius-channel components. Their role is purely quantitative: they convert the published binary lower-risk levels into the normalized expressions used by the finite-range bracket.

Lemma 15 [lem:capped-exact-transport-package] (Capped exact transport scale).

For every overlap level \(0<\epsilon<1/2\), there exist constants \(b_{\epsilon}^{\mathrm{ex}},c_{\epsilon}^{\mathrm{ex}}>0\) and a cutoff \(N_{\epsilon}^{\mathrm{ex}}\in\mathbb N\) such that the following properties hold.

  • (Capped alphabet.) For every pair of integers \(n,d\ge1\), define the exact capped alphabet size from Algorithm 4 by \[d_{\mathrm{ex}} = \min\left\{d,\max\left\{1,\left\lfloor b_{\epsilon}^{\mathrm{ex}}n^2\right\rfloor\right\}\right\}.\] Then \(0<d_{\mathrm{ex}}\le d\), and whenever \(N_{\epsilon}^{\mathrm{ex}}\le n\), \[d_{\mathrm{ex}}\le b_{\epsilon}^{\mathrm{ex}}n^2 .\]

  • (Estimator-wise source risk.) For every \(n,d\ge1\), with \(d_{\mathrm{ex}}\) defined above, there is a real number \(L_{\mathrm{ex}}=L_{\mathrm{ex}}(n,d)\) such that every measurable estimator \[\widehat\psi:\bigl(\{1,\ldots,n\}\to \bigl(\{1,\ldots,d_{\mathrm{ex}}\}\times\{0,1\}\times\{0,1\}\bigr)\bigr)\to\mathbb R\] admits an exactly homogeneous binary law \(R\) over \(d_{\mathrm{ex}}\) cells at overlap \(\epsilon\) for which \[L_{\mathrm{ex}} \le \mathbb E_{Q_R^{(n)}}\!\left[ \{\widehat\psi-\psi(R)\}^2 \right].\]

  • (Transport normalization.) For every \(n,d\ge1\), with the same \(L_{\mathrm{ex}}(n,d)\), and for every \(M\in\mathbb R\), \[c_{\epsilon}^{\mathrm{ex}}M^2 \left\{\frac1n+\min\left(1,\frac d{n^2}\right)\right\} \le M^2L_{\mathrm{ex}} .\]

⊢ Lean
Proof of Lemma 15.

Fix \(\epsilon\in(0,1/2)\). Let \(a_\epsilon,b_\epsilon>0\) and \(N_\epsilon\in\mathbb N\) be the constants in Lemma 4. Set \[b_{\epsilon}^{\mathrm{ex}}:=\min\{b_\epsilon,1/2\}, \qquad K_\epsilon:=1+N_\epsilon+\frac1{b_{\epsilon}^{\mathrm{ex}}},\] \[c_{\epsilon}^{\mathrm{ex}} := \min\left\{\frac{a_\epsilon b_{\epsilon}^{\mathrm{ex}}}{4}, \frac{1}{200K_\epsilon}\right\}, \qquad N_{\epsilon}^{\mathrm{ex}} := \max\left\{N_\epsilon,\left\lceil\frac1{b_{\epsilon}^{\mathrm{ex}}}\right\rceil\right\}.\] The two real constants have the asserted signs, and \(N_{\epsilon}^{\mathrm{ex}}\) is a natural cutoff.

For \(n,d\ge1\), put \[x:=b_{\epsilon}^{\mathrm{ex}}n^2, \qquad d_{\mathrm{ex}} := \min\{d,\max\{1,\lfloor x\rfloor\}\}.\] Since \(d\ge1\), immediately \[0<d_{\mathrm{ex}}\le d .\] If \(N_{\epsilon}^{\mathrm{ex}}\le n\), then \(1/b_{\epsilon}^{\mathrm{ex}}\le n\), so \(b_{\epsilon}^{\mathrm{ex}}n\ge1\). Together with \(n\ge1\), this gives \(1\le x\), and therefore \[d_{\mathrm{ex}} \le \max\{1,\lfloor x\rfloor\} = \lfloor x\rfloor \le x = b_{\epsilon}^{\mathrm{ex}}n^2 .\]

It remains to construct \(L_{\mathrm{ex}}\). First suppose \[N_\epsilon\le n \qquad\text{and}\qquad 1\le x .\] Then \[d_{\mathrm{ex}}=\min\{d,\lfloor x\rfloor\} \quad\text{and}\quad d_{\mathrm{ex}}\le b_\epsilon n^2,\] because \(b_{\epsilon}^{\mathrm{ex}}\le b_\epsilon\). Define \[s_{\mathrm{ex}}:=\frac1n+\frac{d_{\mathrm{ex}}}{n^2}, \qquad L_{\mathrm{ex}}:=\frac{a_\epsilon}{2}s_{\mathrm{ex}} .\] The quantity \(s_{\mathrm{ex}}\) is positive. Hence \(L_{\mathrm{ex}}\) lies strictly below \(a_\epsilon s_{\mathrm{ex}}\), and Lemma 4 gives \[a_\epsilon s_{\mathrm{ex}} \le \inf_{\widehat\psi} \sup_{R\in\mathcal H_{n,d_{\mathrm{ex}}}^{\mathrm{bin}}(\epsilon)} \mathbb E_{Q_R^{(n)}}\!\left[ \{\widehat\psi-\psi(R)\}^2 \right].\] Since \(L_{\mathrm{ex}}\) is strictly below the displayed minimax lower bound, the definition of the infimum and supremum implies that every measurable estimator has some exactly homogeneous binary source law \(R\) over \(d_{\mathrm{ex}}\) cells such that \[L_{\mathrm{ex}} \le \mathbb E_{Q_R^{(n)}}\!\left[ \{\widehat\psi-\psi(R)\}^2 \right].\]

In this large-source case, \[\frac{b_{\epsilon}^{\mathrm{ex}}}{2} \left\{\frac1n+\min\left(1,\frac d{n^2}\right)\right\} \le \frac1n+\frac{d_{\mathrm{ex}}}{n^2}.\] If \(d\le\lfloor x\rfloor\), then \(d_{\mathrm{ex}}=d\) and \(d/n^2\le1\). If \(d>\lfloor x\rfloor\), then \(d_{\mathrm{ex}}=\lfloor x\rfloor\) and \(\lfloor x\rfloor\ge x/2\), so \(d_{\mathrm{ex}}/n^2\ge b_{\epsilon}^{\mathrm{ex}}/2\); together with \(b_{\epsilon}^{\mathrm{ex}}/2\le1\), this absorbs both terms on the left.

Since \(c_{\epsilon}^{\mathrm{ex}}\le a_\epsilon b_{\epsilon}^{\mathrm{ex}}/4\), the preceding display gives, for every \(M\in\mathbb R\), \[c_{\epsilon}^{\mathrm{ex}}M^2 \left\{\frac1n+\min\left(1,\frac d{n^2}\right)\right\} \le M^2L_{\mathrm{ex}} .\]

Now consider the complementary case: either \(n<N_\epsilon\) or \(x<1\). Define \[L_{\mathrm{ex}}:=\frac1{200n}.\] Since \(d_{\mathrm{ex}}>0\), the exact binary source class over \(d_{\mathrm{ex}}\) cells contains the following two uniform-mass endpoint laws. In both laws the propensity is \(1/2\) in every cell and the control-arm binary mean is \(1/2\) in every cell. Under \(R_0\), the treated-arm binary mean is \(1/2\); under \(R_1\), it is \(1/2+\delta\), where \[\delta:=\frac{2}{5\sqrt n}, \qquad \delta^2=\frac{4}{25n}.\] The inequality \(n\ge1\) gives \(0<\delta\le2/5\), so both endpoint laws are valid binary laws with overlap \(\epsilon\) and a common cell effect. Their source targets satisfy \[\psi(R_0)=0, \qquad \psi(R_1)=\delta .\] Moreover \[n\,\frac{(1/2)\delta^2}{(1/2)(1-1/2)} = \frac{8}{25} \le \log 2,\] so the Bernoulli-product total-variation bound gives \[\operatorname{TV}\!\left(Q_{R_0}^{(n)},Q_{R_1}^{(n)}\right)\le\frac12 .\] The two-point testing bound therefore gives the exact-source minimax inequality \[\frac1{100n} = \frac{\delta^2}{16} \le \inf_{\widehat\psi} \sup_{R\in\mathcal H_{n,d_{\mathrm{ex}}}^{\mathrm{bin}}(\epsilon)} \mathbb E_{Q_R^{(n)}}\!\left[ \{\widehat\psi-\psi(R)\}^2 \right].\] Because \(1/(200n)<1/(100n)\), the same infimum-supremum argument yields: every measurable estimator has some exactly homogeneous binary source law \(R\) over \(d_{\mathrm{ex}}\) cells satisfying \[L_{\mathrm{ex}} \le \mathbb E_{Q_R^{(n)}}\!\left[ \{\widehat\psi-\psi(R)\}^2 \right].\]

In this fallback case, \[\frac1n+\min\left(1,\frac d{n^2}\right) \le \frac{K_\epsilon}{n}.\] Indeed, if \(n<N_\epsilon\), then \(N_\epsilon/n\ge1\); if \(x<1\), then \(1/(b_{\epsilon}^{\mathrm{ex}}n)\ge1\). In either case the right side \[\frac{K_\epsilon}{n} = \frac1n+\frac{N_\epsilon}{n} +\frac1{b_{\epsilon}^{\mathrm{ex}}n}\] dominates \(n^{-1}+1\), and the displayed bound follows.

Because \(c_{\epsilon}^{\mathrm{ex}}\le 1/(200K_\epsilon)\), this gives \[c_{\epsilon}^{\mathrm{ex}}M^2 \left\{\frac1n+\min\left(1,\frac d{n^2}\right)\right\} \le M^2\frac1{200n} = M^2L_{\mathrm{ex}} .\] The two cases supply the same capped alphabet properties, the estimator-wise source risk, and the transport normalization for all \(n,d\ge1\).

∎

The exact transport package expresses the binary exactly homogeneous collision lower bound at the scale of the real-outcome target. The capped alphabet \(d_{\mathrm{ex}}\) keeps the embedded source within the available cells, while the normalization clause yields the baseline \(M^2\{n^{-1}+\min(1,d/n^2)\}\).

Lemma 16 [lem:capped-radial-transport-scale] (Capped radial transport scale).

Let \(n,d\) be integers and let \(a,b,M,\sigma\in\mathbb R\). Suppose that

  • (Sample size.) \(n\ge 3\).

  • (Alphabet size.) \(d\ge 1\).

  • (Scale.) \(a\ge 0\).

  • (Radial cap.) \(0<b\le 4\).

  • (Range.) \(1\le (b/2)n\log(en)\).

Define the capped radial alphabet size from Algorithm 4 by \[d_{\mathrm{rad}} := \min\left\{ d,\, \max\left\{1,\left\lfloor (b/2)n\log(en)\right\rfloor\right\} \right\}.\] Then \[\frac{ab^2}{128}M^2\sigma^2 \min\left\{1,\frac{d^2}{n^2\log^2(en)}\right\} \le \left(\frac{M\sigma}{2}\right)^2 \frac a2 \left\{ \frac1n+ \frac{d_{\mathrm{rad}}^2}{n^2\log^2 n} \right\}.\]

⊢ Lean
Proof of Lemma 16.

Let \[L:=\log(en), \qquad x:=\frac b2\,nL, \qquad d_{\mathrm{rad}} = \min\left\{d,\max\{1,\lfloor x\rfloor\}\right\}.\] Since \(n\ge3\), both \(\log n\) and \(L\) are positive, \(L\ge\log n\), and the denominator \(nL\) is positive. The range assumption gives \(1\le x\), hence \(\lfloor x\rfloor>0\) and \(\max\{1,\lfloor x\rfloor\}=\lfloor x\rfloor\).

First, \[\frac b4\min\left\{1,\frac d{nL}\right\} \le \frac{d_{\mathrm{rad}}}{nL}.\] If \(d\le\lfloor x\rfloor\), then \(d_{\mathrm{rad}}=d\). The fraction \(d/(nL)\) is nonnegative, so \(\min\{1,d/(nL)\}\) is nonnegative and at most \(d/(nL)\). Together with \(b/4\le1\), this gives \[\frac b4\min\left\{1,\frac d{nL}\right\} \le \min\left\{1,\frac d{nL}\right\} \le \frac d{nL} = \frac{d_{\mathrm{rad}}}{nL}.\] If \(d>\lfloor x\rfloor\), then \(d_{\mathrm{rad}}=\lfloor x\rfloor\). Since \(x\ge1\), \(\lfloor x\rfloor\ge x/2\), and positivity of \(nL\) gives \[\frac{d_{\mathrm{rad}}}{nL} \ge \frac{x/2}{nL} = \frac b4.\] Also \(\min\{1,d/(nL)\}\le1\) and \(b/4\ge0\), so the desired inequality follows in this case as well.

Both sides of the preceding inequality are nonnegative, hence squaring gives \[\left( \frac b4\min\left\{1,\frac d{nL}\right\} \right)^2 \le \left(\frac{d_{\mathrm{rad}}}{nL}\right)^2.\] It remains to rewrite the squared minimum. Since \(d/(nL)\ge0\), \[\left(\min\left\{1,\frac d{nL}\right\}\right)^2 = \min\left\{1,\frac{d^2}{n^2L^2}\right\}.\] Indeed, if \(d/(nL)\le1\), then the left minimum is \(d/(nL)\), and nonnegativity gives \((d/(nL))^2\le1\). If \(1\le d/(nL)\), then the left minimum is \(1\), and nonnegativity gives \(1\le(d/(nL))^2\). Using also \((d/(nL))^2=d^2/(n^2L^2)\), the squared inequality becomes \[\frac{b^2}{16} \min\left\{1,\frac{d^2}{n^2L^2}\right\} \le \frac{d_{\mathrm{rad}}^2}{n^2L^2}.\] Since \(L\ge\log n>0\), \[\frac{d_{\mathrm{rad}}^2}{n^2L^2} \le \frac{d_{\mathrm{rad}}^2}{n^2\log^2 n}.\] Adding the nonnegative term \(1/n\) on the right therefore yields \[\frac{b^2}{16} \min\left\{1,\frac{d^2}{n^2\log^2(en)}\right\} \le \frac1n+ \frac{d_{\mathrm{rad}}^2}{n^2\log^2 n}.\]

Multiplying this inequality by the nonnegative factor \[\left(\frac{M\sigma}{2}\right)^2\frac a2\] and simplifying constants yields \[\frac{ab^2}{128}M^2\sigma^2 \min\left\{1,\frac{d^2}{n^2\log^2(en)}\right\} \le \left(\frac{M\sigma}{2}\right)^2 \frac a2 \left\{ \frac1n+ \frac{d_{\mathrm{rad}}^2}{n^2\log^2 n} \right\}.\]

∎

Lemma 16 translates the source lower-risk scale into the rare-cell expression \(M^2\sigma^2\min\{1,d^2/(n^2\log^2(en))\}\). The capped \(d_{\mathrm{rad}}\) matches the radius-channel construction in Algorithm 4.

Small sample sizes are absorbed by a separate finite-sample comparison. This keeps the radius-channel lower bound calibrated uniformly over the range in which the asymptotic radial cap is inactive.

Lemma 17 [lem:finite-sample-radial-transport-scale] (Finite-sample radial scale).

Let \(n,d,N\in\mathbb N\) satisfy \(1\le n\le N\), and let \(M,\sigma\in\mathbb R\). Define \[u_{n,d}=\frac{d^2}{n^2\log^2(en)}.\] Then \[\frac{1}{800N}\,M^2\sigma^2\min\{1,u_{n,d}\} \le \left(\frac{M\sigma}{2}\right)^2\frac{1}{200n}.\]

⊢ Lean
Proof of Lemma 17.

Put \[u_{n,d}=\frac{d^2}{n^2\log^2(en)} .\] The minimum is bounded by its first entry, so \[\min\{1,u_{n,d}\}\le 1 .\] The assumptions \(1\le n\le N\) imply \(N>0\). Hence \[0\le \frac{1}{800N}M^2\sigma^2,\] since the remaining factors are squares. Multiplying the preceding minimum bound by this nonnegative factor gives \[\frac{1}{800N}M^2\sigma^2\min\{1,u_{n,d}\} \le \frac{1}{800N}M^2\sigma^2 .\]

The same assumptions give \[\frac1N\le \frac1n .\] Multiplying this reciprocal bound by the nonnegative factors \(1/800\), \(M^2\), and \(\sigma^2\) yields \[\frac{1}{800N}M^2\sigma^2 \le \frac{1}{800n}M^2\sigma^2 .\]

Finally, \[\frac{1}{800n}M^2\sigma^2 = \left(\frac{M\sigma}{2}\right)^2\frac{1}{200n},\] which proves the claim.

∎

Together, Lemmas 16 and 17 supply the scale algebra behind the finite-range radius-channel converse. The converse itself combines the transported radial component with the parametric and exact-homogeneity components.

Theorem 8 [thm:radius-channel-converse] (Radius-channel converse).

For every overlap parameter \(\epsilon\) satisfying \(0<\epsilon<1/2\), there exist constants \(c_{\epsilon}\) and \(b_{\epsilon}^{\mathrm{rad}}\) such that \[0<c_{\epsilon}\le 1, \qquad 0<b_{\epsilon}^{\mathrm{rad}}.\] For every \(n,d\in\mathbb N\), \(M\in\mathbb R\), and \(\sigma\in\mathbb R\), suppose that

  • (Sample and alphabet.) \(0<n\) and \(0<d\).

  • (Scale and radius.) \(1\le M\) and \(0\le\sigma\le2\).

  • (Restricted range.) \[d\le c_{\epsilon}\frac{n^2}{\log(en)}.\]

Then the minimax risk from Definition 5 satisfies \[\mathsf R_{n,d,\epsilon,M,\sigma} \ge c_{\epsilon}M^2 \left\{ \frac1n+\frac d{n^2} +\sigma^2 \min\left(1,\frac{d^2}{n^2\log^2(en)}\right) \right\}.\] Moreover, the radius-channel hard family can be realized with the same constants: there are an integer cap, a source class, a padding map into the \(d\)-alphabet, an embedding \(\Psi\) of the source laws into real-outcome laws, and a coupling for the binary full-observation experiment such that the realized radial-channel data have radius parameter \(b_{\epsilon}^{\mathrm{rad}}\), and for all source laws \(P_0\) and \(P_1\), \[\tau(\Psi(P_1))-\tau(\Psi(P_0)) = M\frac{\sigma}{2} \left\{\psi(P_1)-\psi(P_0)\right\}.\] For every total measurable \([-M,M]\)-valued estimator \(\widehat\tau\), some source law \(P\) in this realized family satisfies \[c_{\epsilon}M^2\sigma^2 \min\left(1,\frac{d^2}{n^2\log^2(en)}\right) \le \mathbb E_{\Psi(P)} \left[ \left\{\widehat\tau-\tau(\Psi(P))\right\}^2 \right].\]

⊢ Lean

Theorem 8 establishes the finite-range lower benchmark used in the original calibrated bracket. Its first two terms give the parametric and collision baseline, and the final term is the radius-channel contribution. The displayed source-target identity records the mechanism of the transport: source contrasts in \(\psi\) become real-outcome contrasts in \(\tau\) through the attenuation \(\sigma/2\) and the outcome scale \(M\), as in classical two-point and fuzzy-hypothesis lower-bound arguments (Le Cam, 1986; Tsybakov, 2009; Cai et al., 2011; Donoho et al., 1990).

Combining the finite-range converse with the constructive selector upper bound gives the restricted-range bracket. This statement is the calibrated predecessor of the all-alphabet bracket in Theorem 4.

Theorem 9 [thm:two-sided-minimax-bracket] (Two-sided minimax bracket).

For every overlap parameter \(\epsilon\) with \(0<\epsilon<1/2\), there exist real constants \(c_{\epsilon}\) and \(C_{\epsilon}\) such that \[0<c_{\epsilon}\le 1,\qquad 1\le C_{\epsilon},\qquad c_{\epsilon}\le C_{\epsilon}.\] For every pair of positive integers \(n,d\) and every \(M,\sigma\in\mathbb R\), assume:

  • (Scale.) \(1\le M\).

  • (Radius.) \(0\le \sigma\le 2\).

  • (Range.) \[d\le \frac{c_{\epsilon}n^2}{\log(en)}.\]

Let \(\mathsf R_{n,d,\epsilon,M,\sigma}\) be the minimax mean-squared risk in Definition 5. Then \[c_{\epsilon}M^2\left\{ \frac{1}{n}+\frac{d}{n^2} +\sigma^2\min\left(1,\frac{d^2}{n^2\log^2(en)}\right) \right\} \le \mathsf R_{n,d,\epsilon,M,\sigma} \le C_{\epsilon}M^2\left\{ \frac{1}{n} +\min\left(1,\min\left(\frac{d^2}{n^2\log^2(en)},\,\sigma^2+\frac{d}{n^2}\right)\right) \right\}.\]

⊢ Lean

Theorem 9 places the finite-range upper and lower benchmarks on the same model class and loss scale. The upper expression is the selector rate, while the lower expression adds the exact-homogeneity baseline to the radius-sensitive rare-cell component. This form is useful for comparisons that keep \(d\) below the displayed calibrated range.

The endpoint reductions identify the two boundary cases of the finite-range bracket. Exact homogeneity reduces the selector and converse expressions to the collision rate, while radius two aligns the radius-indexed class with the unrestricted-radius class.

Proposition 5 [prop:endpoint-reductions] (Endpoint rate reductions).

For every overlap parameter \(\epsilon\in(0,1/2)\), there exist constants \(c_\epsilon,c,C\in\mathbb R\) such that \[0<c_\epsilon\le 1, \qquad 0<c\le C .\] Write \[L_n=\log(en), \qquad u_{n,d}=\frac{d^2}{n^2L_n^2}.\] Then the following statements hold.

  • (Exact-homogeneity endpoint.) For all integers \(n,d\ge1\) such that \[d\le c_\epsilon\,\frac{n^2}{L_n},\] the upper-frontier expression \[\frac1n+\min\left\{1,\min\left\{u_{n,d},\frac d{n^2}\right\}\right\}\] and the capped converse expression \[\frac1n+\min\left\{1,\frac d{n^2}\right\}\] are both comparable to \(n^{-1}+d/n^2\): \[c\left(\frac1n+\frac d{n^2}\right) \le \frac1n+\min\left\{1,\min\left\{u_{n,d},\frac d{n^2}\right\}\right\} \le C\left(\frac1n+\frac d{n^2}\right),\] and \[c\left(\frac1n+\frac d{n^2}\right) \le \frac1n+\min\left\{1,\frac d{n^2}\right\} \le C\left(\frac1n+\frac d{n^2}\right).\]

  • (Radius-two class identity.) For every integer \(d\) and every \(M\in\mathbb R\), the radius-two class in Definition 1 and the unrestricted class in Definition 2 have exactly the same attainable laws: every law in \(\mathcal P_{d,\epsilon,M}^{\mathrm{unr}}\) is the law of some member of \(\mathcal P_{d,\epsilon,M,2}\), and every law in \(\mathcal P_{d,\epsilon,M,2}\) is the law of some member of \(\mathcal P_{d,\epsilon,M}^{\mathrm{unr}}\).

  • (Radius-two endpoint.) For all integers \(n,d\ge1\), the upper-frontier expression at radius two is exactly \[\frac1n+\min\{1,u_{n,d}\},\] and the capped converse expression at radius two, \[\frac1n+\min\left\{1,\frac d{n^2}\right\}+4\min\{1,u_{n,d}\},\] is comparable to the same rate: \[c\left(\frac1n+\min\{1,u_{n,d}\}\right) \le \frac1n+\min\left\{1,\frac d{n^2}\right\}+4\min\{1,u_{n,d}\} \le C\left(\frac1n+\min\{1,u_{n,d}\}\right).\]

⊢ Lean

Proposition 5 gives the finite-range endpoint algebra behind the rate discussion. At \(\sigma=0\), the polynomial branch and the collision lower benchmark reduce to \(n^{-1}+d/n^2\) within the calibrated range. At \(\sigma=2\), the radius-indexed law class equals the unrestricted-radius class from Definition 2, and the upper expression is the polynomial large-alphabet rate.

The final result in this appendix localizes the regimes in which the finite-range bracket is order matched. It uses the baseline \(b_{n,d}\), the polynomial scale \(u_{n,d}\), the triangular selector benchmark \(q_{n,d,\sigma}\), and the product-form triangular benchmark \(\ell^{\mathrm{tri}}_{n,d,\sigma}\). The theorem statement below introduces the local shorthand \(\ell_{n,d,\sigma}\) for that product-form benchmark.

Theorem 10 [thm:fixed-interior-tightness-and-shrinking-radius-gap] (Fixed-interior gap characterization).

For every overlap parameter \(\epsilon\) with \(0<\epsilon<1/2\), there is a constant \(c_\epsilon>0\) such that the following statements hold. Write \[L_n:=\log(en),\qquad b_{n,d}:=\frac1n+\frac d{n^2},\qquad u_{n,d}:=\frac{d^2}{n^2L_n^2},\] and \[q_{n,d,\sigma}:=\frac1n+\min\left\{u_{n,d},\,\sigma^2+\frac d{n^2}\right\}, \qquad \ell_{n,d,\sigma}:=b_{n,d}+\sigma^2u_{n,d}.\] Also let \(r_{n,d,\sigma}\) denote the frontier rate \[r_{n,d,\sigma}:=\frac1n+\min\left\{1,\,\min\{u_{n,d},\,\left(\sigma^2+\frac d{n^2}\right)\}\right\},\] and let \(\mathsf R_{n,d,\epsilon,M,\sigma}\) be the minimax mean-squared risk in Definition 5.

  • (Triangular benchmark algebra.) For all \(n,d\in\mathbb N\) and all \(\sigma\in[0,2]\), if \[L_n^2<d<nL_n,\] then \[\frac12\left\{b_{n,d}+\min(u_{n,d},\sigma^2)\right\} \le q_{n,d,\sigma} \le b_{n,d}+\min(u_{n,d},\sigma^2), \qquad \ell_{n,d,\sigma}=b_{n,d}+\sigma^2u_{n,d}.\]

  • (Fixed interior radii.) For every \(\underline\sigma\in(0,2]\), there are constants \(c,C\) with \(0<c\le C\) such that, for all \(n,d\in\mathbb N\) and \(M,\sigma\in\mathbb R\), if \[n>0,\qquad d>0,\qquad M\ge1,\qquad \underline\sigma\le\sigma\le2,\qquad d\le c_\epsilon\frac{n^2}{L_n},\] then \[cM^2r_{n,d,\sigma} \le \mathsf R_{n,d,\epsilon,M,\sigma} \le CM^2r_{n,d,\sigma}.\]

  • (Matching elbows.) For every \(K\ge0\), there are constants \(c,C\) with \(0<c\le C\) such that, for all \(n,d\in\mathbb N\) and \(M,\sigma\in\mathbb R\), if \[n>0,\qquad d>0,\qquad M\ge1,\qquad 0\le\sigma\le2,\] and at least one of \[u_{n,d}\ge1,\qquad u_{n,d}\le Kb_{n,d},\qquad \sigma^2\le Kb_{n,d}\] holds, then \[cM^2r_{n,d,\sigma} \le \mathsf R_{n,d,\epsilon,M,\sigma} \le CM^2r_{n,d,\sigma}.\]

  • (Exact algebraic elbow.) For all \(n,d\in\mathbb N\) with \(n>0\), \[u_{n,d}\le b_{n,d} \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad d^2\le nL_n^2+dL_n^2.\]

  • (Residual wedge implication.) For every alphabet sequence \(d_t\) and radius sequence \(\sigma_t\), if eventually \[d_t\ge1,\qquad 0\le\sigma_t\le2,\] and \[\frac{r_{t,d_t,\sigma_t}}{t^{-1}+\min\{1,d_t/t^2\}+\sigma_t^2\min\{1,u_{t,d_t}\}}\longrightarrow\infty,\] then the sequence lies in the residual wedge: \[\frac{b_{t,d_t}}{u_{t,d_t}}\longrightarrow0,\qquad u_{t,d_t}\longrightarrow0,\qquad \frac{b_{t,d_t}}{\sigma_t^2}\longrightarrow0,\qquad \sigma_t^2\longrightarrow0.\]

  • (Witness sequence.) The sequence \(d_n=n\) and \(\sigma_n=L_n^{-1/2}\) lies in the residual wedge. Along this sequence, \[\frac{q_{n,n,L_n^{-1/2}}}{\ell_{n,n,L_n^{-1/2}}} \asymp L_n, \qquad \frac{q_{n,n,L_n^{-1/2}}}{\ell_{n,n,L_n^{-1/2}}}\longrightarrow\infty .\]

⊢ Lean

Theorem 10 gives the finite-range phase algebra supporting the matched-regime discussion in the main text. For every radius bounded below by a positive constant, the minimax risk is comparable to \(M^2r_{n,d,\sigma}\) over the calibrated alphabet range. The same comparison holds at the saturation and parametric-dominance elbows. In the triangular region \(L_n^2<d<nL_n\), the theorem separates the selector expression \(b_{n,d}+\min(u_{n,d},\sigma^2)\) from the converse expression \(b_{n,d}+\sigma^2u_{n,d}\), and the witness sequence shows that the residual shrinking-radius region has logarithmic benchmark separation.
